\\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{50\\%}\n& Global & \\textbf{11.79} & \\textbf{32.85} & \\textbf{63.46} & \\textbf{28.1$\\pm$0.2} & 1.29 \\\\\n& Uniform & 11.92$\\pm$0.07 & 32.76 & 62.90 & 28.0$\\pm$0.6 & \\textbf{2.12} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\n\\section{Mask Pattern Visualization}\n\\label{app:mask_viz}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:mask_heatmap} shows per-layer Jaccard distance between KL-derived and magnitude-based masks at 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:scatter} shows per-neuron importance scores at layer 16.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:mask_comparison} shows binary mask comparisons at layers 2, 16, and 28.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{neuron_scatter_layer16.pdf}\n\\caption{KL vs.\\ magnitude importance scores for layer 16 neurons at 50\\% sparsity. 41\\% of neurons fall in disagreement quadrants where one method selects a neuron the other discards.}\n\\label{fig:scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{mask_divergence_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer Jaccard distance between KL-derived and magnitude-based masks at 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity (64 samples, 131K tokens). Divergence peaks in layers 24--28 (Jaccard distance $>$0.73 at 50\\%) and increases with sparsity across all layers.}\n\\label{fig:mask_heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{mask_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary mask comparison between KL-derived and magnitude-based masks at selected layers (L2, L16, L28) for 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity (64 samples). Black = selected by both, light regions = selected by only one method. Deep layers show the largest divergence (L28 Jaccard distance: 0.60 at 30\\%, 0.74 at 50\\%).}\n\\label{fig:mask_comparison}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Architecture Comparison}\n\\label{app:cross_arch}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_arch} evaluates on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (GQA + sliding window, dense PPL 5.52).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-architecture comparison (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$). Adv = TEAL\\,/\\,KL ratio. \\textbf{Note:} LLaMA results use global top-$k$ allocation; Mistral results use uniform allocation. KL improves PPL ($-$17.3\\%) and commonsense tasks (WG +6.7pp) but MMLU is tied (43.3\\% vs.\\ 43.5\\%); ARC-c shows high seed variance ($\\sigma$=6.4pp); GSM8K reverses.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Mistral-7B-v0.3 downstream evaluation at 30\\% sparsity. $\\pm$: 3-seed mean$\\pm$std ($s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$); TEAL is deterministic (single run). ARC-c exhibits high seed variance ($\\sigma$=6.4pp).}\n\\label{tab:mistral_downstream}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & HS & PIQA & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & 5.52 & 60.2 & 78.7 & 83.2 & 81.9 & 62.4 & 36.5 \\\\ % exp_id: lm_eval_mistral_dense, mistral_dense_hellaswag_piqa\nKL 30\\% & \\textbf{6.93$\\pm$0.19} & \\textbf{44.9$\\pm$6.4} & \\textbf{70.2$\\pm$1.2} & \\textbf{69.7$\\pm$0.8} & \\textbf{74.8$\\pm$0.4} & 43.3$\\pm$1.5 & 8.6$\\pm$2.2 \\\\ % exp_id: mistral_kl30_s42, mistral_kl30_s123, mistral_kl30_s456\nTEAL 30\\% & 8.38 & 41.1 & 63.5 & 60.1 & 72.1 & \\textbf{43.5} & \\textbf{15.9} \\\\ % exp_id: mistral_teal30_downstream\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Taxonomy Discussion}\n\\label{app:taxonomy}\n\n\\paragraph{Correction granularity.}\n\\emph{Static} methods~\\cite{xu2025spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.\n\\emph{Per-token} methods~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward,akhauri2024shadowllm,szatkowski2024d2dmoe} produce a unique mask for each input position, conditioning on the token's hidden state at each layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Training signal coupling.}\n\\emph{Decomposed} objectives (BCE) treat each neuron independently, optimizing per-element classification against an oracle mask.\n\\emph{Intermediate} objectives (MSE on hidden states) capture local coupling within a single layer but not end-to-end output effects.\n\\emph{Coupled} objectives (KL on output distribution) propagate gradients through the full forward pass; each mask decision's gradient reflects its joint effect with all other masks on the final distribution.\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2025spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\\times$ coupled).\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nD2DMoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.\nWe define \\emph{coupling alignment} as whether the training loss optimizes a quantity that composes across layers.\nKL on output logits directly optimizes next-token prediction loss, which does not suffer from error composition; per-layer MSE minimizes reconstruction error independently at each layer, but layer-wise optima do not compose---a mask minimizing FFN output error at layer $\\ell$ may amplify errors in layers $\\ell{+}1 \\ldots L$.\nThe SNR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}) corroborates this: KL achieves the best masks despite $2.3\\times$ lower SNR than BCE, while MSE produces the worst masks.\nThe framework predicts that losses operating on output-level quantities should outperform intermediate-level losses, a hypothesis consistent with our MSE results and testable with alternative output-level objectives.\n\n\\section{Oracle Mask Provenance}\n\\label{app:oracle_provenance}\n\nC1's oracle masks are extracted from a converged KL predictor and binarized via top-$k$; they reflect the specific training trajectory of that predictor (optimizer state, data order, Gumbel noise realization).\nWhether a differently initialized KL predictor would discover equivalent mask patterns---and whether BCE can fully recover those patterns from static targets---remains an open question.\nThe s42/s123/s456 consistency ($\\leq$1.70pp) provides partial evidence of robustness, but does not rule out trajectory-dependent confounds.\n\n\\section{SPON Orthogonality}\n\\label{app:spon}\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2025spon} bias correction is weakly correlated with the KL predictor (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038) and yields only 2.4\\% PPL improvement (12.08$\\to$11.79); the predictor already captures most recoverable signal. % exp_id: spon_decomposition, spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42\nThe decomposition confirms that SPON and the KL predictor operate on largely orthogonal error sources.\n",

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Coupling alignment---optimizing through the output distribution---is the dominant design dimension for learned sparsity: signal type accounts for 97.5\% of the PPL gap at 30\% sparsity, while cross-layer coupling contributes downstream fidelity (3--4\,pp on ARC-c, WinoGrande).
5	A predictor ($<$1\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\%, with KL-discovered masks (46--68\% divergent from magnitude) driving inference quality; however, downstream transfer is task- and scale-dependent.
6	Future work includes N:M structured sparsity and scaling to 70B+.
7
```

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	We present a KL distillation framework for training contextual sparsity predictors in SwiGLU-based language models (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}).
5	The method replaces per-neuron binary classification (BCE) with an output-distribution objective that couples all pruning decisions through the model's forward pass and achieves competitive performance without auxiliary error compensation networks at inference.
6	
7	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
8	\label{sec:method:formulation}
9	
10	Consider a pretrained transformer with $L$ layers, each containing a SwiGLU feed-forward network~\cite{shazeer2020glu}.
11	For layer $\ell$, the FFN computes:
12	\begin{align}
13	\mathbf{y}^{(\ell)} = \bigl(\sigma(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_g^{(\ell)}) \odot \mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_u^{(\ell)}\bigr) \mathbf{W}_d^{(\ell)},
14	\label{eq:swiglu}
15	\end{align}
16	where $\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^{d}$ is the hidden state after attention ($d{=}4096$), $\sigma$ denotes SiLU, $\mathbf{W}_g^{(\ell)}, \mathbf{W}_u^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d_{\text{ff}}}$ and $\mathbf{W}_d^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_{\text{ff}} \times d}$ are the gate, up-projection, and down-projection matrices, and $d_{\text{ff}}{=}14{,}336$.
17	
18	Define the gate activation $\mathbf{g}^{(\ell)} = \sigma(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_g^{(\ell)}) \in \mathbb{R}^{d_{\text{ff}}}$.
19	Each element $g_j^{(\ell)}$ determines the contribution of neuron $j$ to the output.
20	We introduce a binary mask $\mathbf{m}^{(\ell)} \in \{0,1\}^{d_{\text{ff}}}$ that zeros out pruned neurons:
21	\begin{align}
22	\mathbf{y}_{\text{sparse}}^{(\ell)} = \bigl(\mathbf{m}^{(\ell)} \odot \mathbf{g}^{(\ell)} \odot \mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_u^{(\ell)}\bigr) \mathbf{W}_d^{(\ell)}.
23	\label{eq:sparse_ffn}
24	\end{align}
25	Our goal is to learn a mask predictor $f_\theta^{(\ell)}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)}) \to \mathbf{s}^{(\ell)} \in (0,1)^{d_{\text{ff}}}$ that produces per-neuron importance scores from $\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)}$.
26	At inference, the scores are fed to a global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\cite{liu2025teal} that distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly, retaining the $(1{-}\rho) \times L \times d_{\text{ff}}$ highest-scoring neurons model-wide for a target sparsity ratio $\rho$.
27	
28	
29	\subsection{Predictor Architecture}
30	\label{sec:method:predictor}
31	
32	Each layer $\ell$ has an independent two-layer MLP predictor:
33	\begin{align}
34	f_\theta^{(\ell)}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)}) = \text{GELU}(\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_1^{(\ell)}) \, \mathbf{W}_2^{(\ell)},
35	\label{eq:predictor}
36	\end{align}
37	where $\mathbf{W}_1^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d_b}$ and $\mathbf{W}_2^{(\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^{d_b \times d_{\text{ff}}}$ with bottleneck dimension $d_b{=}128$.
38	The predictor takes $\mathbf{x}^{(\ell)}$ (the hidden state after attention, before the FFN) and outputs $d_{\text{ff}}$-dimensional logits $\mathbf{s}^{(\ell)}$, from which the mask is derived.
39	
40	Each predictor has $(4096 \times 128) + (128 \times 14336) = 2.37\text{M}$ parameters.
41	With 32 independent predictors (one per layer), the total overhead is 75.9M parameters, less than 1\% of the 8B base model.
42	The predictors operate independently across layers, requiring no cross-layer communication at inference.
43	
44	
45	\subsection{Gumbel-Sigmoid Relaxation}
46	\label{sec:method:gumbel}
47	
48	The hard mask $m_j = \mathbf{1}[s_j > \text{threshold}]$ is non-differentiable, preventing gradient flow from the KL objective through the mask to the predictor parameters.
49	We apply Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{maddison2017concrete,louizos2018l0,jang2017gumbel} to produce differentiable soft masks:
50	\begin{align}
51	\tilde{m}_j = \sigma_\tau\!\left(\log \frac{s_j}{1 - s_j} + g_1 - g_2\right), \quad g_1, g_2 \sim \text{Gumbel}(0,1),
52	\label{eq:gumbel}
53	\end{align}
54	where $\sigma_\tau(x) = \text{sigmoid}(x / \tau)$ and $\tau$ is a temperature parameter.
55	The Gumbel noise provides stochastic exploration of mask configurations during training, while the temperature controls sharpness: as $\tau \to 0$, $\tilde{m}_j$ approaches a binary decision.
56	
57	We anneal $\tau$ linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over training.
58	At high temperature, the soft mask permits gradient flow through all neurons for broad exploration.
59	As temperature decreases, the mask concentrates on confident decisions, approximating the hard allocation used at inference.
60	
61	\paragraph{Comparison with Straight-Through Estimation.}
62	The straight-through estimator (STE)~\cite{bengio2013ste} applies a hard threshold in the forward pass and passes gradients through unchanged ($\partial m / \partial s = 1$).
63	In our setting, STE produces discontinuous gradient signals: small parameter changes can flip many neurons simultaneously. The resulting oscillations in the penalty weight prevent convergence to the target sparsity.
64	Gumbel relaxation eliminates this pathology and contributes a 33.8\% improvement (PPL 15.77 $\to$ 11.79 at 50\% sparsity), as we verify empirically in \S\ref{sec:experiments}.
65	
66	
67	\subsection{KL Divergence Training Objective}
68	\label{sec:method:kl}
69	
70	Let $\mathbf{z}_{\text{dense}}$ denote the output logits from a full dense forward pass, and $\mathbf{z}_{\text{sparse}}$ the logits from a forward pass with predictor-generated soft masks $\{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{(\ell)}\}_\ell$ applied at every layer.
71	We minimize the forward KL divergence between the dense and sparse output distributions:
72	\begin{align}
73	\mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}} = D_{\text{KL}}(p_{\text{dense}} \,\|\, p_{\text{sparse}}) = \sum_{v} p_{\text{dense}}^{(v)} \log \frac{p_{\text{dense}}^{(v)}}{p_{\text{sparse}}^{(v)}},
74	\label{eq:kl_loss}
75	\end{align}
76	where $p_{\text{dense}} = \text{softmax}(\mathbf{z}_{\text{dense}})$ and $p_{\text{sparse}} = \text{softmax}(\mathbf{z}_{\text{sparse}})$, summed over the vocabulary $v$ and averaged over sequence positions.
77	The base model is frozen; only the predictor parameters receive gradients.
78	
79	We use forward KL (mass-covering) rather than reverse KL, as the goal is to preserve the dense model's primary predictions rather than explore alternative modes.
80	In sparsity prediction, mode-covering is critical: false negatives (omitting a rarely but critically active neuron) cascade through downstream layers under global allocation, whereas false positives merely waste capacity; reverse KL's mode-seeking concentrates on high-probability neurons and risks missing compositionally important activations~\cite{wu2025rethinking}.
81	\S\ref{sec:experiments} validates this choice empirically: forward KL outperforms reverse KL by 8.6\% and JSD by 6.4\% (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}).
82	No temperature scaling ($T{=}1$) is applied~\cite{hinton2015distilling}.
83	
84	
85	\subsection{Adaptive Penalty Controller}
86	\label{sec:method:lagrangian}
87	
88	We enforce a model-level sparsity target $\rho$ via constrained optimization with $\bar{s} = \frac{1}{L} \sum_\ell \frac{1}{d_{\text{ff}}} \sum_j \tilde{m}_j^{(\ell)}$ denoting the average retained fraction:
89	\begin{align}
90	\min_\theta \; \mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}} \quad \text{s.t.} \quad \bar{s} = 1 - \rho.
91	\label{eq:constrained}
92	\end{align}
93	We relax this via an adaptive penalty controller:
94	\begin{align}
95	\mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_{\text{KL}} + \lambda \cdot \text{clamp}(|\bar{s} - (1 - \rho)| - \epsilon, \min{=}0)^2,
96	\label{eq:lagrangian}
97	\end{align}
98	where $\epsilon = 0.02$ is a deadzone margin. The penalty weight $\lambda$ is updated via geometric doubling (doubled when sparsity exceeds target, halved otherwise), clamped to $[\lambda_{\text{floor}}, \lambda_{\max}]$, reaching target sparsity within 40--110 steps across all tested regimes (Appendix~\ref{app:lagrangian}).
99	
100	
101	\subsection{Gradient Analysis: BCE vs.\ KL}
102	\label{sec:method:gradient}
103	
104	To build intuition for how KL distillation discovers neuron importance patterns that diverge from magnitude-based masks (\S\ref{sec:mask_analysis}), we contrast the gradient structure of the two objectives. This analysis is suggestive; \S\ref{sec:mask_analysis} provides evidence that the coupled gradient discovers mask targets that yield lower perplexity, with mask quality driving inference-stage quality.
105	
106	Under BCE, the predictor trains against oracle masks $m_j^* = \mathbf{1}[|g_j| \geq \text{top-}k\text{ threshold}]$ derived from activation magnitudes.
107	The gradient $\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{BCE}} / \partial s_j = \sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$; each neuron is trained independently with no inter-neuron coupling.
108	Under KL distillation, the chain rule introduces a downstream Jacobian $\partial \mathbf{z}_{\text{sparse}} / \partial \tilde{m}_j^{(\ell)}$ that couples neuron $j$'s gradient to all other retained neurons in layers $\ell$ through $L$, because the sparse hidden state propagates through subsequent attention and FFN operations.
109	This coupling allows KL to evaluate each neuron's importance in the context of the full sparse computation, rather than against a fixed magnitude target.
110	
111	Gradient SNR measurements\footnote{$\text{SNR}_\ell = \|\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta_\ell} \mathcal{L}]\|_2^2 / \text{Var}[\nabla_{\theta_\ell} \mathcal{L}]$, expectation over mini-batches.} reveal that BCE maintains consistently higher SNR than KL throughout training ($2.3\times$ at convergence; Figure~\ref{fig:snr_trajectory}), indicating that KL's quality advantage does not arise from cleaner gradient signals.
112	Rather, KL's end-to-end coupled gradient serves as a \emph{search mechanism} that discovers compositionally superior mask patterns, even when the per-step signal is noisier than BCE's decomposed updates.
113	The target-swap ablation in \S\ref{sec:mask_analysis} confirms that the discovered mask targets, not the coupling mechanism itself, drive inference-stage quality.
114	
115	\subsection{Two-Dimensional Taxonomy of Sparsity Prediction Methods}
116	\label{sec:method:taxonomy}
117	
118	We organize prior work along two axes: \emph{correction granularity} (how the mask adapts to input) and \emph{training signal coupling} (whether the objective captures inter-neuron dependencies).
119	
120	\begin{table}[t]
121	\centering
122	\caption{Taxonomy of learned sparsity prediction methods along correction granularity (rows) and training signal coupling (columns). Our method occupies the Per-token $\times$ Coupled cell (organizational framework). Empty cells represent unexplored combinations; their relative merit is an open question.}
123	\label{tab:taxonomy}
124	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
125	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
126	\toprule
127	 & Decomposed (BCE) & Intermediate (MSE) & Coupled (KL) \\
128	\midrule
129	Static & --- & --- & SPON \\
130	Per-token & DejaVu, FastForward & ShadowLLM, D2DMoE & \textbf{Ours} \\
131	\bottomrule
132	\end{tabular}}
133	\end{table}
134	
135	Our method combines per-token prediction with coupled KL training (Table~\ref{tab:taxonomy}).
136	KL on output logits directly optimizes next-token prediction loss, which composes across layers; per-layer MSE does not---a mask minimizing FFN output error at layer $\ell$ may amplify errors in layers $\ell{+}1 \ldots L$, explaining its $1.7\times$/$9.6\times$ worse PPL than TEAL at 30\%/50\% (\S\ref{sec:experiments}). % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42
137	Full taxonomy discussion in Appendix~\ref{app:taxonomy}.
138	
139	
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work proposes a training method for sparsity predictors in pretrained language models, evaluated on publicly available models (LLaMA-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B-v0.3, Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}) and standard benchmarks (WikiText-2, WikiText-103, C4, ARC, WinoGrande, HellaSwag, PIQA, MMLU, GSM8K).\nNo new data collection, annotation, or human subjects are involved.\nThe method does not alter model outputs beyond the sparsity-induced degradation we analyze; it introduces no new generation capabilities or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base models.\nCompute requirements are modest (1000 training steps, $\\sim$45 minutes on a single GPU for KL training), presenting minimal environmental concern relative to the pretrained models we evaluate on.\nWe release no model weights or user-facing systems; our contribution is analytical (understanding KL distillation for sparsity) rather than deployment-oriented.\n\n\\section*{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}\n\n\\paragraph{A. {
```
```json
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "890db0e9-cece-4fde-8b95-b1ac56f184ad",

    "oldString": "A coupling alignment hierarchy emerges",
    "newString": "A coupling alignment ranking emerges",
    "originalFile": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs universally use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train lightweight predictors ($<$1\\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nOn LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with commonsense gains but knowledge-intensive decline; post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation confirms these discovered patterns---not the training mechanism itself---as the proximate quality driver.\nA coupling alignment hierarchy emerges across five loss configurations; a per-layer KL control decomposes this hierarchy into two dimensions: output-distribution alignment drives PPL (per-layer KL within 3\\% of end-to-end at 30\\% sparsity), while cross-layer coupling provides additional downstream benefit (3--4\\,pp on ARC-Challenge and WinoGrande).\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.\n",
```

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "cbb50768-d3d9-451e-9303-441ef8c87fee",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "2e140446-055b-4395-994e-d3ff8167825f",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "d9ba04ec-c250-49cc-b943-1c0ea58fe02e",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "SwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nSwiGLU's gated activation makes prediction challenging: importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.",
    "newString": "How the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity---a property exploitable for potential inference acceleration---but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nSwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nSwiGLU's gated activation makes prediction challenging: importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation confirms that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Cross-layer gradient coupling is not the primary driver of PPL-level quality, but contributes meaningfully to downstream task fidelity---a PPL--downstream dissociation quantified in \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with robust commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. The empirical finding---that coupling alignment with the output distribution is the critical design dimension for learned sparsity---is the paper's central contribution. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item A coupling alignment hierarchy across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (dominant for PPL) and coupling scope (dominant for downstream); output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Preliminary cross-scale evaluation of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 6,
        "lines": [
          " \\section{Introduction}",
          " ",
          "-SwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.",
          "-SwiGLU's gated activation makes prediction challenging: importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.",
          "+How the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity---a property exploitable for potential inference acceleration---but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.",
          " Learned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.",
          " Yet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.",
          " This mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed."
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
```

```json
    "oldString": "\\item A coupling alignment hierarchy across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (dominant for PPL) and coupling scope (dominant for downstream); output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Preliminary cross-scale evaluation of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).",
    "newString": "\\item A coupling alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (dominant for PPL) and coupling scope (dominant for downstream); output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope, with exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity---a property exploitable for potential inference acceleration---but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation confirms that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Cross-layer gradient coupling is not the primary driver of PPL-level quality, but contributes meaningfully to downstream task fidelity---a PPL--downstream dissociation quantified in \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with robust commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. The empirical finding---that coupling alignment with the output distribution is the critical design dimension for learned sparsity---is the paper's central contribution. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item A coupling alignment hierarchy across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (dominant for PPL) and coupling scope (dominant for downstream); output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; cross-layer coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond what PPL captures (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Preliminary cross-scale evaluation of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",

      {

          "+\\item A coupling alignment ranking across five losses, decomposed by a per-layer KL control into signal type (dominant for PPL) and coupling scope (dominant for downstream); output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope, with exploratory cross-scale evidence at 7--14B (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
```

```json
    "oldString": "Crucially, MaskLLM does not investigate \\emph{why} KL training helps; our target-swap ablation disentangles the roles of gradient coupling and discovered mask targets (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).",
    "newString": "The recipe-level principle (frozen weights + learnable masks + KL) overlaps with MaskLLM; our contribution lies in the analysis framework---target-swap ablation, signal type $\\times$ coupling scope decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer})---applied to input-dependent activation sparsity under global allocation.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-Free Activation Sparsity.}\nTEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude and achieves 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.\nCATS~\\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} predicts activation sparsity patterns from attention outputs without additional training.\nWang et al.~\\cite{wang2025dynamicsparse} address the interaction between dynamic sparsity and quantization with zigzag-patterned weight layouts.\nDhar et al.~\\cite{dhar2024activation} characterize activation sparsity opportunities across architectures, showing that SwiGLU models exhibit lower natural sparsity than ReLU counterparts.\nOWL~\\cite{yin2024owl} determines non-uniform per-layer sparsity ratios based on activation outlier distribution for weight pruning.\nAll training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \\emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor-Based Contextual Sparsity.}\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} introduced learned sparsity predictors trained via BCE to classify each neuron independently.\nShadowLLM~\\cite{akhauri2024shadowllm} uses attention-based routing with per-layer MSE supervision for intermediate coupling within each layer but without end-to-end gradient signal.\nFastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} adds a 67.2M-parameter compensation network using BCE for mask prediction, adding inference latency.\nSVD-CSP~\\cite{serbin2026svdcsp} compresses sparsity predictors via SVD decomposition for reduced predictor overhead.\nDhar et al.~\\cite{dhar2025clustering} compress activation patterns via clustering for efficient neuron-level prediction.\nPowerInfer~\\cite{song2024powerinfer} exploits power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU scheduling.\nMaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} shares the principle of frozen weights + learnable masks + KL training, but the two methods differ in three fundamental ways.\nFirst, \\emph{sparsity type}: MaskLLM targets N:M semi-structured \\emph{weight} sparsity (static 2:4 patterns), while we target \\emph{activation} sparsity (dynamic per-token masks). Weight masks are fixed after training; our masks vary with each input token.\nSecond, \\emph{mask generation}: MaskLLM's masks are directly learnable parameters (one per weight group) selected via Gumbel-Softmax; we train a predictor network that generates masks at inference from hidden states for input-dependent decisions.\nThird, \\emph{allocation}: MaskLLM optimizes within fixed N:M groups where KL coupling operates only within small local neighborhoods; our masks are ranked under a global top-$k$ budget across all layers, creating long-range dependencies that per-neuron BCE cannot capture and amplifying the coupled-vs-decomposed gap.\nCrucially, MaskLLM does not investigate \\emph{why} KL training helps; our target-swap ablation disentangles the roles of gradient coupling and discovered mask targets (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).\nAll prior predictor-based methods for activation sparsity use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nOur contribution is primarily empirical: we observe that a coupled KL objective propagates gradients through all downstream layers and quantify when this matters for global allocation quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Static Bias Correction.}\nSPON (Spontaneous Neurons;~\\citealp{xu2025spon}) learns input-independent bias vectors via closed-form KL minimization per layer, absorbed into weights at deployment.\nOur method is fundamentally different: a per-token contextual predictor trained end-to-end across all layers that produces input-dependent masks.\nSPON solves a static per-layer problem; we solve a global dynamic prediction problem. The two are weakly correlated (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\paragraph{Activation Function Engineering.}\nReLU Strikes Back~\\cite{mirzadeh2024relu} replaces SiLU with ReLU for $>$90\\% natural sparsity; TurboSparse~\\cite{song2024turbosparse} and ProSparse~\\cite{song2025prosparse} engineer extreme sparsity through activation modifications; the Sparsing Law~\\cite{luo2024sparsinglaw} formalizes how activation density increases with training compute.\nMiniTron~\\cite{muralidharan2024minitron} combines structured pruning with distillation but modifies architecture permanently.\nHaziza et al.~\\cite{haziza2025accelerating} demonstrate 2:4 semi-structured activation sparsity with hardware-native acceleration, complementing our unstructured approach.\nMoC~\\cite{wu2025moc} selectively activates Top-$K$ FFN channels using SwiGLU's native gating during pretraining.\nAll require retraining or weight modification; our method is a plug-in predictor on \\emph{unmodified} SwiGLU models.\n\n\\paragraph{Weight Pruning, Distillation, and Routing.}\nWanda~\\cite{sun2024wanda}, SparseGPT~\\cite{frantar2023sparsegpt}, and SliceGPT~\\cite{ashkboos2024slicegpt} produce static weight masks via one-shot or structured pruning; Polar Sparsity~\\cite{shrestha2025polar} scales contextual sparsity to batched inference by shifting focus from MLP to attention head sparsity.\nSwitch Transformers~\\cite{fedus2022switch} and Mixture-of-Depths~\\cite{raposo2024mod} achieve conditional computation through learned routing.\nKL-based distillation~\\cite{kim2016sequencekd,sanh2019distilbert,gu2024minillm,agarwal2024gkd,ko2024distillm} trains compact students; SparseLoRA~\\cite{khaki2025sparselora} exploits contextual sparsity for fine-tuning.\nWe apply the KL principle to train a sparsity predictor on unmodified dense models, using Gumbel-Sigmoid sampling~\\cite{maddison2017concrete,louizos2018l0} for differentiable discrete mask optimization.\n\n\\paragraph{MoE Routing and Gated Sparsity.}\nExpert Choice routing~\\cite{zhou2022expertchoice} lets each expert select its top-$k$ tokens under a fixed capacity budget, a global allocation perspective that parallels TEAL's neuron-level global top-$k$.\nD2D-MoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} converts dense SwiGLU layers into dynamic-$k$ MoE by exploiting the same activation sparsity we target, but restructures the network into discrete experts rather than predicting masks on the original architecture.\nMoNE~\\cite{cheng2025mone} applies neuron-granular top-$k$ selection within each MoE expert during pretraining for fine-grained conditional computation without additional routing parameters.\n",
```

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "62e35092-f3a5-41ce-be30-9d59589c5973",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "PowerInfer~\\cite{song2024powerinfer} exploits power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU scheduling.",
    "newString": "PowerInfer~\\cite{song2024powerinfer} and PowerInfer-2~\\cite{xue2024powerinfer2} exploit power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU/NPU scheduling.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-Free Activation Sparsity.}\nTEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude and achieves 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.\nCATS~\\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} predicts activation sparsity patterns from attention outputs without additional training.\nWang et al.~\\cite{wang2025dynamicsparse} address the interaction between dynamic sparsity and quantization with zigzag-patterned weight layouts.\nDhar et al.~\\cite{dhar2024activation} characterize activation sparsity opportunities across architectures, showing that SwiGLU models exhibit lower natural sparsity than ReLU counterparts.\nOWL~\\cite{yin2024owl} determines non-uniform per-layer sparsity ratios based on activation outlier distribution for weight pruning.\nAll training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \\emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor-Based Contextual Sparsity.}\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} introduced learned sparsity predictors trained via BCE to classify each neuron independently.\nShadowLLM~\\cite{akhauri2024shadowllm} uses attention-based routing with per-layer MSE supervision for intermediate coupling within each layer but without end-to-end gradient signal.\nFastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} adds a 67.2M-parameter compensation network using BCE for mask prediction, adding inference latency.\nSVD-CSP~\\cite{serbin2026svdcsp} compresses sparsity predictors via SVD decomposition for reduced predictor overhead.\nDhar et al.~\\cite{dhar2025clustering} compress activation patterns via clustering for efficient neuron-level prediction.\nPowerInfer~\\cite{song2024powerinfer} exploits power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU scheduling.\nMaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} shares the principle of frozen weights + learnable masks + KL training, but the two methods differ in three fundamental ways.\nFirst, \\emph{sparsity type}: MaskLLM targets N:M semi-structured \\emph{weight} sparsity (static 2:4 patterns), while we target \\emph{activation} sparsity (dynamic per-token masks). Weight masks are fixed after training; our masks vary with each input token.\nSecond, \\emph{mask generation}: MaskLLM's masks are directly learnable parameters (one per weight group) selected via Gumbel-Softmax; we train a predictor network that generates masks at inference from hidden states for input-dependent decisions.\nThird, \\emph{allocation}: MaskLLM optimizes within fixed N:M groups where KL coupling operates only within small local neighborhoods; our masks are ranked under a global top-$k$ budget across all layers, creating long-range dependencies that per-neuron BCE cannot capture and amplifying the coupled-vs-decomposed gap.\nThe recipe-level principle (frozen weights + learnable masks + KL) overlaps with MaskLLM; our contribution lies in the analysis framework---target-swap ablation, signal type $\\times$ coupling scope decomposition (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer})---applied to input-dependent activation sparsity under global allocation.\nAll prior predictor-based methods for activation sparsity use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nOur contribution is primarily empirical: we observe that a coupled KL objective propagates gradients through all downstream layers and quantify when this matters for global allocation quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Static Bias Correction.}\nSPON (Spontaneous Neurons;~\\citealp{xu2025spon}) learns input-independent bias vectors via closed-form KL minimization per layer, absorbed into weights at deployment.\nOur method is fundamentally different: a per-token contextual predictor trained end-to-end across all layers that produces input-dependent masks.\nSPON solves a static per-layer problem; we solve a global dynamic prediction problem. The two are weakly correlated (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\paragraph{Activation Function Engineering.}\nReLU Strikes Back~\\cite{mirzadeh2024relu} replaces SiLU with ReLU for $>$90\\% natural sparsity; TurboSparse~\\cite{song2024turbosparse} and ProSparse~\\cite{song2025prosparse} engineer extreme sparsity through activation modifications; the Sparsing Law~\\cite{luo2024sparsinglaw} formalizes how activation density increases with training compute.\nMiniTron~\\cite{muralidharan2024minitron} combines structured pruning with distillation but modifies architecture permanently.\nHaziza et al.~\\cite{haziza2025accelerating} demonstrate 2:4 semi-structured activation sparsity with hardware-native acceleration, complementing our unstructured approach.\nMoC~\\cite{wu2025moc} selectively activates Top-$K$ FFN channels using SwiGLU's native gating during pretraining.\nAll require retraining or weight modification; our method is a plug-in predictor on \\emph{unmodified} SwiGLU models.\n\n\\paragraph{Weight Pruning, Distillation, and Routing.}\nWanda~\\cite{sun2024wanda}, SparseGPT~\\cite{frantar2023sparsegpt}, and SliceGPT~\\cite{ashkboos2024slicegpt} produce static weight masks via one-shot or structured pruning; Polar Sparsity~\\cite{shrestha2025polar} scales contextual sparsity to batched inference by shifting focus from MLP to attention head sparsity.\nSwitch Transformers~\\cite{fedus2022switch} and Mixture-of-Depths~\\cite{raposo2024mod} achieve conditional computation through learned routing.\nKL-based distillation~\\cite{kim2016sequencekd,sanh2019distilbert,gu2024minillm,agarwal2024gkd,ko2024distillm} trains compact students; SparseLoRA~\\cite{khaki2025sparselora} exploits contextual sparsity for fine-tuning.\nWe apply the KL principle to train a sparsity predictor on unmodified dense models, using Gumbel-Sigmoid sampling~\\cite{maddison2017concrete,louizos2018l0} for differentiable discrete mask optimization.\n\n\\paragraph{MoE Routing and Gated Sparsity.}\nExpert Choice routing~\\cite{zhou2022expertchoice} lets each expert select its top-$k$ tokens under a fixed capacity budget, a global allocation perspective that parallels TEAL's neuron-level global top-$k$.\nD2D-MoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} converts dense SwiGLU layers into dynamic-$k$ MoE by exploiting the same activation sparsity we target, but restructures the network into discrete experts rather than predicting masks on the original architecture.\nMoNE~\\cite{cheng2025mone} applies neuron-granular top-$k$ selection within each MoE expert during pretraining for fine-grained conditional computation without additional routing parameters.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "oldLines": 7,
```

```json
  "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur findings are bounded by three key limitations.\n\\textbf{Sparsity sweet spot.} 30\\% appears to be the lowest-degradation level for downstream fidelity; at 40\\%, MMLU drops to 35.5\\% (vs.\\ 40.7\\% at 30\\%), and at 50\\%, all methods collapse to near-random on reasoning tasks. A separate predictor must be retrained per target.\n\\textbf{Knowledge-intensive regression.} KL consistently underperforms magnitude-based methods on MMLU and GSM8K across scales (8B: $-$5.2pp MMLU; 14B: $-$20pp GSM8K), suggesting distribution-matching objectives systematically sacrifice reasoning-critical pathways.\n\\textbf{Training data homogeneity.} All predictors are trained on WikiText-103 (English Wikipedia). OOD evaluation on C4 shows preserved KL advantage but $31\\times$ higher cross-seed variance (CV=6.3\\% vs.\\ 0.2\\% in-domain), indicating domain sensitivity. Evaluation is limited to 7--14B; scaling to 70B+ and wall-clock speedup via sparse kernels remain future work.\n"
}
```

```json
          " ",
          "-Our primary evaluation uses WikiText-2 perplexity, and downstream benchmarks reveal that perplexity is a poor proxy for reasoning ability at moderate sparsity: 40\\% sparsity achieves PPL 10.76 but MMLU already degrades to 35.5\\% (vs.\\ 40.7\\% at 30\\%); 30\\% appears to be the lowest-degradation sparsity level for downstream fidelity.",
          "-Training requires 1000 steps on WikiText-103 ($\\sim$45 minutes on RTX PRO 6000 for KL, $\\sim$17 minutes for BCE), but a separate predictor must be trained for each sparsity target.",
          "-On Mistral-7B (3-seed), KL improves PPL ($-$17.3\\%) and commonsense tasks (WG, HS, PIQA: all seeds win) but MMLU is tied (43.3\\% vs.\\ 43.5\\%) and GSM8K reverses across all seeds (7.0--11.1\\% vs.\\ TEAL 15.9\\%). Forward KL's mass-covering property may preserve output breadth at the expense of reasoning-critical pathways.",
          "-The 30\\% sparsity analysis is validated at 7--8B scale; at 14B, KL's MMLU decline ($-$2.79pp uniform, $-$8.1pp global) and GSM8K reversal ($-$20pp uniform; 32.1$\\pm$5.2\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 52.1\\%, 3-seed) reflect task-and-scale-dependent effects (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_arch}; Appendix~\\ref{app:14b}): at 8B all methods collapse to $<$9\\% GSM8K, so this reversal emerges only with sufficient base capacity. KL's high cross-seed GSM8K variance ($\\sigma$=5.2pp) suggests minor training perturbations disrupt reasoning-critical pathways that magnitude pruning preserves more robustly.           "-Training data is domain-homogeneous: all predictors are trained on WikiText-103 (English Wikipedia), a single-domain corpus.",
          "-Activation importance patterns may differ on code, multilingual, or dialogue data; the predictor could underperform on domains with substantially different neuron utilization profiles.",

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur primary evaluation uses WikiText-2 perplexity, and downstream benchmarks reveal that perplexity is a poor proxy for reasoning ability at moderate sparsity: 40\\% sparsity achieves PPL 10.76 but MMLU already degrades to 35.5\\% (vs.\\ 40.7\\% at 30\\%); 30\\% appears to be the lowest-degradation sparsity level for downstream fidelity.\nTraining requires 1000 steps on WikiText-103 ($\\sim$45 minutes on RTX PRO 6000 for KL, $\\sim$17 minutes for BCE), but a separate predictor must be trained for each sparsity target.\nOn Mistral-7B (3-seed), KL improves PPL ($-$17.3\\%) and commonsense tasks (WG, HS, PIQA: all seeds win) but MMLU is tied (43.3\\% vs.\\ 43.5\\%) and GSM8K reverses across all seeds (7.0--11.1\\% vs.\\ TEAL 15.9\\%). ARC-c is seed-dependent (37.8--50.1\\% vs.\\ TEAL 41.1\\%; $\\sigma$=6.4pp vs.\\ LLaMA-8B's 2.5pp), indicating reduced cross-architecture metric stability. Forward KL's mass-covering property may preserve output breadth at the expense of reasoning-critical pathways.\nThe 30\\% sparsity analysis is validated at 7--8B scale; at 14B, KL's MMLU decline ($-$2.79pp uniform, $-$8.1pp global) and GSM8K reversal ($-$20pp uniform; 32.1$\\pm$5.2\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 52.1\\%, 3-seed) reflect task-and-scale-dependent effects (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_arch}; Appendix~\\ref{app:14b}): at 8B all methods collapse to $<$9\\% GSM8K, so this reversal emerges only with sufficient base capacity. KL's high cross-seed GSM8K variance ($\\sigma$=5.2pp) suggests minor training perturbations disrupt reasoning-critical pathways that magnitude pruning preserves more robustly. Scaling to 70B remains untested.\nTraining data is domain-homogeneous: all predictors are trained on WikiText-103 (English Wikipedia), a single-domain corpus.\nActivation importance patterns may differ on code, multilingual, or dialogue data; the predictor could underperform on domains with substantially different neuron utilization profiles.\nOut-of-distribution evaluation on C4 (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}) shows the KL advantage transfers but with a wider gap: C4 PPL at 30\\% is $1.74\\times$ dense vs.\\ $1.46\\times$ on in-domain WikiText-2, which indicates that the predictor partially overfits to training-domain activation patterns. Downstream task performance on C4 shows all six benchmarks within 1.1pp of in-domain results (Table~\\ref{tab:ood_downstream}), though this is single-seed (s42) only. Extending OOD evaluation to 70\\% sparsity is left to future work.\nThe target-swap ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}) confirms that mask quality drives inference-stage quality, with KL's coupled gradients necessary at the discovery stage. C1 demonstrates mask transferability but does not fully resolve whether gradient coupling has additional benefits beyond target discovery. The mechanism by which KL's coupled gradient discovers lower-perplexity mask patterns during training remains incompletely characterized; future work could trace per-layer mask divergence trajectories across training to identify when and where the patterns diverge.\nAt 30\\% sparsity, global allocation exhibits 9.5pp cross-seed MMLU standard deviation (3-seed); allocation strategy interacts with predictor initialization in task-specific ways. At 8B, this variance is MMLU-specific under global allocation, as ARC, WinoGrande, and GSM8K show stable performance across seeds ($\\sigma$$\\leq$0.7pp); at 14B, GSM8K variance emerges ($\\sigma$=5.2pp, above).\nWhile we motivate our work with inference speedup potential, actual wall-clock acceleration depends on sparse kernel implementations (e.g., Triton, cuSPARSE) and hardware-specific memory access patterns; this work focuses on prediction quality rather than systems optimization.\nFuture directions include N:M structured sparsity for hardware-native acceleration, scaling to 13B+ models, and training a single predictor that serves multiple sparsity targets simultaneously.\n",
```

```json
          " \\label{sec:perlayer}",
          " To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask one random layer per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.",
          "-Per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse). Signal type\\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} accounts for 97.5\\% of the PPL gap. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed",
          "-However, downstream tasks reveal a dissociation: per-layer KL underperforms end-to-end KL by 3--5 pp on commonsense (ARC-c, WinoGrande, HellaSwag), while BoolQ shows the largest gap: per-layer KL achieves 64.5$\\pm$11.5\\% vs.\\ 73.7$\\pm$3.2\\% for E2E KL (both 3-seed, uniform), a 9.2\\,pp deficit with $3.6\\times$ higher seed variance, suggesting cross-layer coupling is critical for stable binary reasoning. At 50\\%, coupling scope's importance increases sharply: per-layer KL degrades to 34.85 vs.\\ 11.92 for E2E KL (+192\\%), with coupling's share rising from 2.5\\% to 10.6\\% ($4\\times$; Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}) and downstream falling to TEAL level---coupling transitions from optional refinement to essential coordination as sparsity increases. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_downstream, perlayer_kl_50_s42",
          "+Per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse).           " The per-layer KL comparison isolates coupling scope but introduces a train--inference distribution shift: each training step masks only one random layer, while inference applies all 32 masks simultaneously.
```
